I just graduated with my bachelor's, and I've been applying for things and helping out with some research, but... I keep making these really careless mistakes. \n\nWhat finally made me come in is that I keep turning in applications or research notes with small but embarrassing errors. Like, I'll mislabel an artwork date, or I'll just completely skip a question on a form. And the frustrating part is, I honestly thought I had checked everything over before I submitted it. It's just happening too often.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you were paying attention when you were reviewing your work before submitting it?\n\nPatient: I really thought I was. Like, I sit there and look over the page, and I'm reading the words. But... I don't know. I guess maybe my mind just glosses over things? It's hard to say, because in the moment it feels like I'm paying full attention, but then obviously I missed something right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety while you're working on these tasks?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't feel restless or like I need to get up and move around. I can sit still at my desk for a long time while I'm working on applications or doing research. It's just that my mind isn't always fully on what I'm doing, even when I'm sitting right there. But physically, no, I don't really fidget or feel antsy.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention in other areas of your life, outside of work and research?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I haven't really noticed it outside of that. Like, if I'm just going about my day, or hanging out, or watching a movie, I seem to pay attention just fine. It really just seems to be an issue when I have to sit down and do that kind of focused, detailed work for my career stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted by your surroundings or thoughts while working on these detailed tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. When I'm trying to read or write at my desk, it really doesn't take much to pull me away. Like, if I hear a siren outside or just someone talking out in the hallway, I'll lose my focus. \n\nAnd it's not just stuff around me, it's my own thoughts too. I might get a random thought about an exhibit idea or something, and my mind just goes with it. I can easily get derailed for twenty minutes before I even notice I stopped working.\n\nDoctor: Are these mistakes and difficulties with focused work causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it's a little annoying when I realize I've wasted time, but it doesn't cause me a lot of stress or anxiety. It's been going on for as long as I can remember. I'd miss all these little details unless my parents went through my backpack every single night to check my work. It really just seems to be an issue when I have to sit down and do that kind of focused, detailed work for my career stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted by your surroundings or thoughts while working on these detailed tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. When I'm trying to read or write at my desk, it really doesn't take much to pull me away. Like, if I hear a siren outside or just someone talking out in the hallway, I'll lose my focus. \n\nAnd it's not just stuff around me, it's my own thoughts too. I might get a random thought about an exhibit idea or something, and my mind just goes with it. I can easily get derailed for twenty minutes before I even notice I stopped working.\n\nDoctor: Are these mistakes and difficulties with focused work causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it's a little annoying when I realize I've wasted time, but it doesn't cause me a lot of stress or anxiety. It's more of just a frustration with myself in the moment, rather than something that really weighs on me or makes me anxious. I just kind of sigh and try to get back to work.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with careful attention to detail, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not new. It's been going on for as long as I can remember. Even back in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I had good ideas, but I was always forgetting to turn in worksheets or losing instructions. I'd miss all these little details unless my parents went through my backpack every single night to check my work. So yeah, it's always been a thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety in situations where you're not working, such as when you're relaxing or socializing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel that way. I mean, I'm pretty comfortable just sitting and relaxing, or hanging out with friends. I don't get restless or feel the need to move around a lot when I'm just chilling. It's mostly just the focus part that's an issue for me, not so much being fidgety.\n\nDoctor: Were you often restless or fidgety as a child?\n\nPatient: No, not really. From what I remember, and from what my parents have told me, I was actually a pretty quiet kid. I wasn't the type to be bouncing off the walls or constantly running around. I was more likely to just be staring out the window or daydreaming. So, no, I wouldn't say I was restless or fidgety back then either.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or materials when working on projects or assignments?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. Um, my apartment and my laptop both basically look like a bunch of half-started projects right now. There's just... rent papers, gallery notes, article PDFs, and all these different to-do lists everywhere. When I'm looking at all of it, I just have a really hard time figuring out what actually needs to happen first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I struggle with that. I'm not the type to just jump into things without thinking them through. If anything, I usually get stuck thinking too much about what I'm supposed to do, rather than just doing something on impulse. So, no, I wouldn't say I do that.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more energy than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um, like I mentioned a minute ago, I was actually a pretty quiet kid. I definitely didn't have more energy than the other kids my age. I wasn't running around or wearing people out... I was usually just kind of in my own head or doing my own thing. So, no, I wouldn't say I was overly energetic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following instructions or paying attention in classes or meetings?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't think so, no. I mean, I can usually follow instructions fine if they're clear. And in classes, I don't really have a major problem paying attention, at least not more than anyone else, I guess. I might zone out sometimes if it's really boring, but I wouldn't say it's a big issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever get in trouble in school for getting out of your seat or blurring out answers?\n\nPatient: No, I never got in trouble for anything like that. Like I mentioned earlier, I was a pretty quiet kid. I wasn't the type to jump out of my seat or yell out answers... I mostly just stayed put and kept to myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I just kind of sigh and try to get back to work.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with careful attention to detail, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not new. It's been going on for as long as I can remember. Even back in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I had good ideas, but I was always forgetting to turn in worksheets or losing instructions. I'd miss all these little details unless my parents went through my backpack every single night to check my work. So yeah, it's always been a thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety in situations where you're not working, such as when you're relaxing or socializing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel that way. I mean, I'm pretty comfortable just sitting and relaxing, or hanging out with friends. I don't get restless or feel the need to move around a lot when I'm just chilling. It's mostly just the focus part that's an issue for me, not so much being fidgety.\n\nDoctor: Were you often restless or fidgety as a child?\n\nPatient: No, not really. From what I remember, and from what my parents have told me, I was actually a pretty quiet kid. I wasn't the type to be bouncing off the walls or constantly running around. I was more likely to just be staring out the window or daydreaming. So, no, I wouldn't say I was restless or fidgety back then either.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or materials when working on projects or assignments?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. Um, my apartment and my laptop both basically look like a bunch of half-started projects right now. There's just... rent papers, gallery notes, article PDFs, and all these different to-do lists everywhere. When I'm looking at all of it, I just have a really hard time figuring out what actually needs to happen first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I struggle with that. I'm not the type to just jump into things without thinking them through. If anything, I usually get stuck thinking too much about what I'm supposed to do, rather than just doing something on impulse. So, no, I wouldn't say I do that.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more energy than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um, like I mentioned a minute ago, I was actually a pretty quiet kid. I definitely didn't have more energy than the other kids my age. I wasn't running around or wearing people out... I was usually just kind of in my own head or doing my own thing. So, no, I wouldn't say I was overly energetic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following instructions or paying attention in classes or meetings?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't think so, no. I mean, I can usually follow instructions fine if they're clear. And in classes, I don't really have a major problem paying attention, at least not more than anyone else, I guess. I might zone out sometimes if it's really boring, but I wouldn't say it's a big issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever get in trouble in school for getting out of your seat or blurring out answers?\n\nPatient: No, I never got in trouble for anything like that. Like I mentioned earlier, I was a pretty quiet kid. I wasn't the type to jump out of my seat or yell out answers... I mostly just stayed put and kept to myself.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with attention to detail and organization affecting your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'd say it mostly affects things on the professional side, or with people I work with. Because of my issues with organization, I’ve submitted work late, and I actually missed a couple of application deadlines recently. I even had a mentor tell me that my lack of follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for. So, it's definitely causing some friction there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel impatient or have trouble waiting for others in social situations?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I'm particularly impatient. I don't mind waiting my turn in lines or when I'm out with friends. I don't really feel an urge to rush people or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I'm here because I've been having some trouble lately with my work. I just graduated with my bachelor's, and I've been applying for things and helping out with some research, but... I keep making these really careless mistakes. \n\nWhat finally made me come in is that I keep turning in applications or research notes with small but embarrassing errors. Like, I'll mislabel an artwork date, or I'll just completely skip a question on a form. And the frustrating part is, I honestly thought I had checked everything over before I submitted it. It's just happening too often.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you were paying attention when you were reviewing your work before submitting it?\n\nPatient: I really thought I was. Like, I sit there and look over the page, and I'm reading the words. But... I don't know. I guess maybe my mind just glosses over things? It's hard to say, because in the moment it feels like I'm paying full attention, but then obviously I missed something right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety while you're working on these tasks?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't feel restless or like I need to get up and move around. I can sit still at my desk for a long time while I'm working on applications or doing research. It's just that my mind isn't always fully on what I'm doing, even when I'm sitting right there. But physically, no, I don't really fidget or feel antsy.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention in other areas of your life, outside of work and research?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I haven't really noticed it outside of that. Like, if I'm just going about my day, or hanging out, or watching a movie, I seem to pay attention just fine. It really just seems to be an issue when I have to sit down and do that kind of focused, detailed work for my career stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted by your surroundings or thoughts while working on these detailed tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. When I'm trying to read or write at my desk, it really doesn't take much to pull me away. Like, if I hear a siren outside or just someone talking out in the hallway, I'll lose my focus. \n\nAnd it's not just stuff around me, it's my own thoughts too. I might get a random thought about an exhibit idea or something, and my mind just goes with it. I can easily get derailed for twenty minutes before I even notice I stopped working.\n\nDoctor: Are these mistakes and difficulties with focused work causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it's a little annoying when I realize I've wasted time, but it doesn't cause me a lot of stress or anxiety. It's more of just a frustration with myself in the moment, rather than something that really weighs on me or makes me anxious. I just kind of sigh and try to get back to work.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with careful attention to detail, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not new. It's been going on for as long as I can remember. Even back in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I had good ideas, but I was always forgetting to turn in worksheets or losing instructions. I'd miss all these little details unless my parents went through my backpack every single night to check my work. So yeah, it's always been a thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety in situations where you're not working, such as when you're relaxing or socializing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel that way. I mean, I'm pretty comfortable just sitting and relaxing, or hanging out with friends. I don't get restless or feel the need to move around a lot when I'm just chilling. It's mostly just the focus part that's an issue for me, not so much being fidgety.\n\nDoctor: Were you often restless or fidgety as a child?\n\nPatient: No, not really. From what I remember, and from what my parents have told me, I was actually a pretty quiet kid. I wasn't the type to be bouncing off the walls or constantly running around. I was more likely to just be staring out the window or daydreaming. So, no, I wouldn't say I was restless or fidgety back then either.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or materials when working on projects or assignments?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. Um, my apartment and my laptop both basically look like a bunch of half-started projects right now. There's just... rent papers, gallery notes, article PDFs, and all these different to-do lists everywhere. When I'm looking at all of it, I just have a really hard time figuring out what actually needs to happen first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I struggle with that. I'm not the type to just jump into things without thinking them through. If anything, I usually get stuck thinking too much about what I'm supposed to do, rather than just doing something on impulse. So, no, I wouldn't say I do that.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more energy than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um, like I mentioned a minute ago, I was actually a pretty quiet kid. I definitely didn't have more energy than the other kids my age. I wasn't running around or wearing people out... I was usually just kind of in my own head or doing my own thing. So, no, I wouldn't say I was overly energetic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following instructions or paying attention in classes or meetings?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't think so, no. I mean, I can usually follow instructions fine if they're clear. And in classes, I don't really have a major problem paying attention, at least not more than anyone else, I guess. I might zone out sometimes if it's really boring, but I wouldn't say it's a big issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever get in trouble in school for getting out of your seat or blurring out answers?\n\nPatient: No, I never got in trouble for anything like that. Like I mentioned earlier, I was a pretty quiet kid. I wasn't the type to jump out of my seat or yell out answers... I mostly just stayed put and kept to myself.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with attention to detail and organization affecting your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'd say it mostly affects things on the professional side, or with people I work with. Because of my issues with organization, I’ve submitted work late, and I actually missed a couple of application deadlines recently. I even had a mentor tell me that my lack of follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for. So, it's definitely causing some friction there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel impatient or have trouble waiting for others in social situations?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I'm particularly impatient. I don't mind waiting my turn in lines or when I'm out with friends. I don't really feel an urge to rush people or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have difficulty engaging in activities quietly as a child?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I'm here because I've been having some trouble lately with my work. I just graduated with my bachelor's, and I've been applying for things and helping out with some research, but... I keep making these really careless mistakes. \n\nWhat finally made me come in is that I keep turning in applications or research notes with small but embarrassing errors. Like, I'll mislabel an artwork date, or I'll just completely skip a question on a form. And the frustrating part is, I honestly thought I had checked everything over before I submitted it. It's just happening too often.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you were paying attention when you were reviewing your work before submitting it?\n\nPatient: I really thought I was. Like, I sit there and look over the page, and I'm reading the words. But... I don't know. I guess maybe my mind just glosses over things? It's hard to say, because in the moment it feels like I'm paying full attention, but then obviously I missed something right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety while you're working on these tasks?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't feel restless or like I need to get up and move around. I can sit still at my desk for a long time while I'm working on applications or doing research. It's just that my mind isn't always fully on what I'm doing, even when I'm sitting right there. But physically, no, I don't really fidget or feel antsy.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention in other areas of your life, outside of work and research?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I haven't really noticed it outside of that. Like, if I'm just going about my day, or hanging out, or watching a movie, I seem to pay attention just fine. It really just seems to be an issue when I have to sit down and do that kind of focused, detailed work for my career stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted by your surroundings or thoughts while working on these detailed tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. When I'm trying to read or write at my desk, it really doesn't take much to pull me away. Like, if I hear a siren outside or just someone talking out in the hallway, I'll lose my focus. \n\nAnd it's not just stuff around me, it's my own thoughts too. I might get a random thought about an exhibit idea or something, and my mind just goes with it. I can easily get derailed for twenty minutes before I even notice I stopped working.\n\nDoctor: Are these mistakes and difficulties with focused work causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it's a little annoying when I realize I've wasted time, but it doesn't cause me a lot of stress or anxiety. It's more of just a frustration with myself in the moment, rather than something that really weighs on me or makes me anxious. I just kind of sigh and try to get back to work.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with careful attention to detail, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not new. It's been going on for as long as I can remember. Even back in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I had good ideas, but I was always forgetting to turn in worksheets or losing instructions. I'd miss all these little details unless my parents went through my backpack every single night to check my work. So yeah, it's always been a thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety in situations where you're not working, such as when you're relaxing or socializing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel that way. I mean, I'm pretty comfortable just sitting and relaxing, or hanging out with friends. I don't get restless or feel the need to move around a lot when I'm just chilling. It's mostly just the focus part that's an issue for me, not so much being fidgety.\n\nDoctor: Were you often restless or fidgety as a child?\n\nPatient: No, not really. From what I remember, and from what my parents have told me, I was actually a pretty quiet kid. I wasn't the type to be bouncing off the walls or constantly running around. I was more likely to just be staring out the window or daydreaming. So, no, I wouldn't say I was restless or fidgety back then either.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or materials when working on projects or assignments?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. Um, my apartment and my laptop both basically look like a bunch of half-started projects right now. There's just... rent papers, gallery notes, article PDFs, and all these different to-do lists everywhere. When I'm looking at all of it, I just have a really hard time figuring out what actually needs to happen first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I struggle with that. I'm not the type to just jump into things without thinking them through. If anything, I usually get stuck thinking too much about what I'm supposed to do, rather than just doing something on impulse. So, no, I wouldn't say I do that.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more energy than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um, like I mentioned a minute ago, I was actually a pretty quiet kid. I definitely didn't have more energy than the other kids my age. I wasn't running around or wearing people out... I was usually just kind of in my own head or doing my own thing. So, no, I wouldn't say I was overly energetic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following instructions or paying attention in classes or meetings?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't think so, no. I mean, I can usually follow instructions fine if they're clear. And in classes, I don't really have a major problem paying attention, at least not more than anyone else, I guess. I might zone out sometimes if it's really boring, but I wouldn't say it's a big issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever get in trouble in school for getting out of your seat or blurring out answers?\n\nPatient: No, I never got in trouble for anything like that. Like I mentioned earlier, I was a pretty quiet kid. I wasn't the type to jump out of my seat or yell out answers... I mostly just stayed put and kept to myself.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with attention to detail and organization affecting your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'd say it mostly affects things on the professional side, or with people I work with. Because of my issues with organization, I’ve submitted work late, and I actually missed a couple of application deadlines recently. I even had a mentor tell me that my lack of follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for. So, it's definitely causing some friction there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel impatient or have trouble waiting for others in social situations?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I'm particularly impatient. I don't mind waiting my turn in lines or when I'm out with friends. I don't really feel an urge to rush people or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have difficulty engaging in activities quietly as a child?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't. Like I said, I was a really quiet kid. I didn't have any trouble playing quietly or just doing my own thing without making a lot of noise.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with attention to detail and organization causing problems in your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, like I said, it's mostly just on the professional or academic side. I haven't really noticed it causing any big issues with my friends or family. They haven't complained about it or anything, so... yeah, outside of those work and school things, it doesn't really cause problems for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or have trouble waiting for your turn when you were a child?\n\nPatient: No, not that I remember. Um, since I was such a quiet kid, I didn't really interrupt people or talk over them. And waiting my turn was always fine for me. I usually just kind of hung back and waited.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like, I sit there and look over the page, and I'm reading the words. But... I don't know. I guess maybe my mind just glosses over things? I don't feel restless or like I need to get up and move around. I can sit still at my desk for a long time while I'm working on applications or doing research. It's just that my mind isn't always fully on what I'm doing, even when I'm sitting right there. I haven't really noticed it outside of that. Like, if I'm just going about my day, or hanging out, or watching a movie, I seem to pay attention just fine. When I'm trying to read or write at my desk, it really doesn't take much to pull me away. I might get a random thought about an exhibit idea or something, and my mind just goes with it. I can easily get derailed for twenty minutes before I even notice I stopped working.\n\nDoctor: Are these mistakes and difficulties with focused work causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it's a little annoying when I realize I've wasted time, but it doesn't cause me a lot of stress or anxiety. It's more of just a frustration with myself in the moment, rather than something that really weighs on me or makes me anxious. I just kind of sigh and try to get back to work.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with careful attention to detail, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not new. It's been going on for as long as I can remember. Even back in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I had good ideas, but I was always forgetting to turn in worksheets or losing instructions. I'd miss all these little details unless my parents went through my backpack every single night to check my work. I mean, I'm pretty comfortable just sitting and relaxing, or hanging out with friends. I don't get restless or feel the need to move around a lot when I'm just chilling. It's mostly just the focus part that's an issue for me, not so much being fidgety.\n\nDoctor: Were you often restless or fidgety as a child?\n\nPatient: No, not really. From what I remember, and from what my parents have told me, I was actually a pretty quiet kid. I wasn't the type to be bouncing off the walls or constantly running around. I was more likely to just be staring out the window or daydreaming. So, no, I wouldn't say I was restless or fidgety back then either.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or materials when working on projects or assignments?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. Um, my apartment and my laptop both basically look like a bunch of half-started projects right now. There's just... rent papers, gallery notes, article PDFs, and all these different to-do lists everywhere. I don't think I struggle with that. I'm not the type to just jump into things without thinking them through. If anything, I usually get stuck thinking too much about what I'm supposed to do, rather than just doing something on impulse. So, no, I wouldn't say I do that.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more energy than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um, like I mentioned a minute ago, I was actually a pretty quiet kid. I definitely didn't have more energy than the other kids my age. I wasn't running around or wearing people out... I was usually just kind of in my own head or doing my own thing. So, no, I wouldn't say I was overly energetic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following instructions or paying attention in classes or meetings?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't think so, no. I mean, I can usually follow instructions fine if they're clear. And in classes, I don't really have a major problem paying attention, at least not more than anyone else, I guess. I might zone out sometimes if it's really boring, but I wouldn't say it's a big issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever get in trouble in school for getting out of your seat or blurring out answers?\n\nPatient: No, I never got in trouble for anything like that. Like I mentioned earlier, I was a pretty quiet kid. I wasn't the type to jump out of my seat or yell out answers... I mostly just stayed put and kept to myself.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with attention to detail and organization affecting your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'd say it mostly affects things on the professional side, or with people I work with. Because of my issues with organization, I’ve submitted work late, and I actually missed a couple of application deadlines recently. I even had a mentor tell me that my lack of follow-through is starting to hurt opportunities that I should be ready for. So, it's definitely causing some friction there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel impatient or have trouble waiting for others in social situations?\n\nPatient: No, not really.
